We exemplify the method for Borel-Moore homology and singular cohomology, but it can be applied in many other situations such as equivariant Chow groups, see loc. cit., approximation of the algebraic or topological Chern character, see loc. cit. and Subsection
5.3
, or vanishing cohomology, see
[
DM20
, Subsection 2.2]
.

Let
\mathscr{X}=X/G
be a quotient stack with
G
an algebraic group and
X
quasi-projective scheme with a
G
-linearized
action.
We choose
G
-representations

\{V_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with surjections

V_{n}\twoheadrightarrow V_{n-1}
such that
the closed subset
S_{n}\subset V_{n}

of points with non-trivial stabilizers
has codimension at least
n
. Further we may choose
V_{n}
such that, for
U_{n}:=V_{n}\setminus S_{n}
, the quotient
U_{n}/G
is a scheme
[
EG98
, Lemma 9]
.
Then the quotient
(X\times U_{n})/G
is also a scheme because
X
is quasi-projective
[
EG98
, Proposition 23]
.
For
\ell
fixed and for
n
large enough, there are isomorphisms of Borel-Moore homologies
induced by pullbacks

(5.4)

\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\ell}(\mathscr{X})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\ell+2\dim V_{n}}((X\times V_{n})/G)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\ell+2\dim V_{n}}\left((X\times U_{n})/G\right).

We also have isomorphisms of cohomologies
induced by pull-backs

(5.5)

\displaystyle H^{\ell}(\mathscr{X})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\ell}((X\times V_{n})/G)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\ell}\left((X\times U_{n})/G\right).

5.3.
The Chern character map for a classical quotient stack

Let
\mathscr{X}=X/G
be a quotient stack,
where
G
is a connected reductive group and
X
is a classical quasi-projective scheme with an action of
G
. Let
M
be a compact Lie group such that
G
is the complexification of
M
.
Let
EM
be a contractible CW complex with a free action of
M
. For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
the Chern character map of the CW complex
EM\times_{M}X

defines the Chern character map for
\mathscr{X}
:

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\colon G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})

\displaystyle=G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(EM\times_{M}X)

\displaystyle\to\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(EM\times_{M}X)=\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}):=\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i+2j}(\mathscr{X}).

Remark 5.3
.

The above Chern character map is, in general, neither injective nor surjective. It becomes an isomorphism when we take the completion of the
the K-group with respect to an augmentation ideal by theorems of Atiyah–Segal
[
AS69
]
, and Edidin–Graham
[
EG00
]
in the algebraic case.

The Chern character map for a stack can be approximated by the Chern character map for varieties as follows
[
EG00
]
, see also Subsection
5.2
.
For
V
a representation of
G
, denote by
S\subset V
the closed set of points with non-trivial stabilizers. Let
U:=V\setminus S
. We may choose
V
such that
U/G
and
(X\times U)/G
are schemes.
Then the following diagram commutes, where the vertical maps are pullback maps and the bottom map is an isomorphism
over
\mathbb{Q}
by the Atiyah-Hirzebruch theorem:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}((X\times V)/G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}((X\times V)/G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}\left((X\times U)/G\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}\left((X\times U)/G\right).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{V}}

Let
\{V_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

be
G
-representations
with surjections
V_{n}\twoheadrightarrow V_{n-1}
,
and take closed subsets
S_{n}\subset V_{n}
as in Subsection
5.2
.
For
\ell
fixed and for
n
large enough, recall that
we have isomorphisms (
5.4
) induced by pullbacks.
Then
\mathrm{ch}(y)

for
y\in G_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X})

equals the limit

(5.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{ch}(y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\operatorname{ch}_{V_{n}}(\mathrm{res}_{V_{n}}(y)).

Remark 5.4
.

In the algebraic case, Edidin–Graham show in
[
EG00
, Proposition 3.1]
that the limit of
\mathrm{ch}_{V_{n}}(\mathrm{res}_{V_{n}}(y))
is well-defined and use it to define the Chern character.

In a similar way, there is also a Chern character map for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
:

(5.8)

\mathrm{ch}\colon K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})\to\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X}):=\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{i+2j}(\mathscr{X}).

As above, the Chern character map (
5.8
) can be approximated by Chern character maps of varieties.
The Chern character maps (
5.6
) and (
5.8
) are compatible
by the following commutative diagram.

(5.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

Here
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
are the maps induced by intersecting with the fundamental class, see
[
BFM79
, Section 5 and Property 2 from Section 4.1]
:

5.4.
The cycle maps for quotient stacks

Let
\mathscr{X}
be a classical quotient stack as in the previous subsection.
For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we consider the increasing filtration

(5.10)

E_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}):=\mathrm{ch}^{-1}\left(H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\leqslant i+2\ell}(\mathscr{X})\right)\subset G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}).

Note that
E_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})=G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})
for
\ell
large enough and that
E_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})
contains the kernel of the Chern character for all
\ell
.
The associated graded of the above filtration is denoted by

\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}):=E_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})/E_{\ell-1}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}).

The Chern character induces a map, which we call
the cycle map
:

(5.11)

\mathrm{c}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})\to H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i+2\ell}(\mathscr{X}).

Note that the cycle map is injective by construction.

Example 5.5
.

Let
\mathscr{X}=BGL(d)
. Then the Chern character map is

\displaystyle\operatorname{ch}\colon G_{0}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X})=\mathbb{Z}[q_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,q_{d}^{\pm 1}]^{\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\to\widetilde{H}_{0}^{\rm{BM}}(\mathscr{X})=\mathbb{Q}[[h_{1},\ldots,h_{d}]]^{\mathfrak{S}_{d}}

given by
q_{i}\mapsto e^{h_{i}}
.
Let
I\subset\mathbb{Z}[q_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,q_{d}^{\pm 1}]
be the ideal
generated by
q_{i}-1
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
.
The induced filtration on

G_{0}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X})
is

\displaystyle\cdots\subset I^{2}\cap G_{0}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X})\subset I\cap G_{0}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X})\subset G_{0}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X}).

For
\mathscr{X}
a quotient stack as above, recall that there is an intersection product
(
2.2
). We note the following immediate statement.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\alpha\in\widetilde{H}^{0}(\mathscr{X})

be of the form
\alpha=1+\alpha^{\prime}
for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\prod_{i\geqslant 1}H^{i}(\mathscr{X})
.
The twisted Chern character map

\mathrm{ch}^{\prime}(-):=\mathrm{ch}(-)\cdot\alpha

induces a map

\displaystyle c^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}G_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X})\to H_{i+2\ell}^{\rm{BM}}(\mathscr{X})

which equals the cycle map (
5.11
).

5.5.
The Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch theorem

We state the (topological) Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch (GRR) theorem for lci morphisms of (classical and possibly singular) varieties due to Baum–Fulton–MacPherson
[
BFM75
,
BFM79
]
.
Let
\mathscr{X}
be a classical
quotient stack and
\mathscr{X}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}

its closed substack.
Note that
\mathscr{X}^{\prime}
is also a quotient stack.
We consider the topological K-theory and the Borel-Moore homology with supports in
\mathscr{X}^{\prime}
,
denoted by

G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{X})
,
H_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}^{\rm{BM}}(\mathscr{X})

respectively.
We have Chern character maps with fixed supports:

(5.12)

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X}^{\prime},\mathscr{X}}\colon G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{X})\to\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{X}).

Note that by the push-forward along

\mathscr{X}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}
, we have isomorphisms, see
[
HLP20
, Section 2.1.1]
:

(5.13)

\displaystyle G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X}^{\prime})\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{X}),\ H_{\bullet}^{\rm{BM}}(\mathscr{X}^{\prime})\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}H_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}^{\rm{BM}}(\mathscr{X}).

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
be classical quotient stacks and let
f\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
be an lci morphism.
Let
\mathscr{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{Y}
be closed substacks.

(a) Assume that

f^{-1}(\mathscr{Y}^{\prime})\subset\mathscr{X}^{\prime}
. Then the following diagram commutes:

(5.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{X}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

(b) Assume that
f
is proper and

f(\mathscr{X}^{\prime})\subset\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}
.
Let
T_{f}
be the virtual tangent bundle of
f
and consider its Todd class
\mathrm{td}(T_{f})\in\widetilde{H}^{0}(\mathscr{X})
.
By setting

f^{\prime}_{*}(-):=f_{*}(\mathrm{td}(T_{f})\cdot(-))
,
the following diagrams commute:

(5.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{Y}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{*}}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{Y}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{*}}

Proof.

(a) There exist Gysin pull-backs for any quasi-smooth morphism for Borel-Moore homology
[
Khab
, Construction 3.4]
and for derived categories
[
PS23
]
, and thus for topological K-theory by (
5.1
).
Such maps have also been constructed for lci morphisms of classical schemes in
[
BFM75
, Section 4.4]
, see also
[
BFM79
, Section 4.2 and 5]
. The commutativity of the diagram (
5.14
) follows from standard properties of the Chern character
[
BFM79
, Section 5]
.

(b) For
f
a proper and lci morphism,
there exist pushforward maps
f_{*}\colon K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X})\to K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Y})
and
f_{*}\colon H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X})\to H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Y})
, see
[
BFM75
, Section 4.4]
,
[
BFM79
, Section 5, Remark (2)]
.
The diagrams commute by the Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch
theorem for lci morphisms, see
[
BFM79
, Section 5, Remark (2)]
(note that there are typos in the statement of the diagram for
K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}
, see
[
Khaa
]
for a statement in the algebraic case). These are the topological versions of the usual algebraic GRR theorems, see
[
BFM75
, Section 4.3]
,
[
Khaa
]
.

Note that Baum–Fulton–MacPherson state the above theorems only for
\bullet=0
because they are interested in stating a result for
K^{\mathrm{alg}}_{0}
or
G^{\mathrm{alg}}_{0}
obtained by composing with the natural map to
K^{\mathrm{top}}_{0}
or
G^{\mathrm{top}}_{0}
, respectively. However, the same proofs (based on deformation to the normal cone and the excess intersection formula) apply for
\bullet=1
as well. Finally, note that Baum-Fulton-MacPherson treat the case when
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
are schemes, but the extension to stacks is obtained using the approximation from Subsection
5.2
, see also Subsection
5.3
.
∎

5.6.
The Chern character map for quasi-smooth stacks

Let
\mathscr{X}=X/G
be a quotient stack with
X
a quasi-smooth derived
scheme.
Here we construct a Chern character map for
\mathscr{X}
.
One can define such a map using the Chern character map for
\mathscr{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
and the isomorphisms (
5.2
). However, we define a Chern character map which takes account of
the derived structure of
\mathscr{X}
.

Suppose that there is a closed immersion
i\colon\mathscr{X}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{Y}
, where
\mathscr{Y}
is a smooth classical quotient stack.
Let
N
be the normal bundle of
i
, which is a vector bundle on
\mathscr{X}
and thus has a Todd class
\mathrm{td}(N)\in\widetilde{H}^{0}(\mathscr{X})
.
Let
\operatorname{ch}_{\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}}
be
the local Chern character map

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}}\colon G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}}(\mathscr{Y})\to\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet,\mathscr{X}}(\mathscr{Y}).

By twisting the local Chern character map
with
\mathrm{td}(N)
and
using the isomorphisms (
5.2
), (
5.13
),
we define the following Chern character map

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X}}:=\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}}\cdot\mathrm{td}(N)\colon G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X})\to\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X}).

Lemma 5.8
.

The map
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X}}
is independent of a choice of
\mathscr{Y}
as above.

Proof.

Let
i^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{X}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}
be another
closed immersion. We take a smooth quotient stack
\mathscr{Y}^{\prime\prime}
and closed immersions
j\colon\mathscr{Y}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{Y}^{\prime\prime}
and
j^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{Y}^{\prime\prime}
. Note that the Todd classes for the normal bundles of
ji
and
j^{\prime}i^{\prime}
are the same.
The statement then follows from the GRR theorem for the closed immersions
j
and
j^{\prime}
, see Theorem
5.7
.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

If
\mathscr{X}
is a classical stack, then the Chern character map constructed above coincides with the usual Chern character
map (
5.12
), as one can see using the
GRR theorem for a closed immersion of
\mathscr{X}
in a smooth ambient stack.

Using Theorem
5.7
, one proves a topological GRR theorem for quasi-smooth morphisms.

Proposition 5.10
.

(i) Let
f\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
be a quasi-smooth proper map of quasi-smooth quotient stacks. Let
T_{f}
be the virtual tangent bundle and consider the Todd class
\mathrm{td}(T_{f})\in\widetilde{H}^{0}(\mathscr{X})
. By setting
f^{\prime}_{*}(-):=f_{*}(\mathrm{td}(T_{f})\cdot(-))
,
the following diagram commutes:

(5.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Y}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{*}}

(ii)
For any smooth morphism
f\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
between quasi-smooth quotient stacks, the following diagram commutes:

(5.18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}_{\mathscr{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

Similarly to (
5.10
),
the Chern character map (
5.16
)
defines a filtration

\displaystyle E_{\ell}G_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X})\subset G_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X})

and the cycle map
from the associated graded

(5.19)

\displaystyle c\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}G_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X})\to H_{i+2\ell}^{\rm{BM}}(\mathscr{X}).

We record the following corollary of the diagrams (
5.17
) and (
5.18
), see also Proposition
5.6
.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
f\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
be a quasi-smooth morphism of quasi-smooth quotient stacks of relative dimension
d
. Let
i,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
.
If
f
is smooth, then it induces a pullback map:

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{Y})\to\mathrm{gr}_{\ell+d}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}).

If
f
is proper, then it induces a pushforward map:

\displaystyle f_{*}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X})\to\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{Y}).

We also note the following lemma to be used later:

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
\mathscr{X}
be a smooth quotient stack and
set
\mathscr{X}[\epsilon]:=\mathscr{X}\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[\epsilon]
,
where
\deg\epsilon=-1
.
Then the natural map

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime}\colon K_{i}^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathscr{X}[\epsilon])\to G_{i}^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathscr{X}[\epsilon])

is the zero map.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be the natural projection
\pi\colon\mathscr{X}[\epsilon]\to\mathscr{X}
and let
l\colon(\mathscr{X}[\epsilon])^{\rm{cl}}=\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{X}[\epsilon]
the closed immersion. Then we have isomorphisms

\displaystyle\pi^{*}\colon K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X})\xrightarrow{\sim}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X}[\epsilon]),\ l_{*}\colon G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X}[\epsilon])\xrightarrow{\sim}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{X}).

For any topological vector bundle
E
on
\mathscr{X}
,
we have

\varepsilon^{\prime}(\pi^{*}(E))=l_{*}(E)\oplus l_{*}(E)[1]=0\in G^{\mathrm{top}}_{0}(\mathscr{X}[\epsilon]).

Therefore the conclusion for
i=0
holds.
A similar computation holds for the suspension of
\mathscr{X}
,
so the conclusion also holds for
i=1
.
∎

6.
Topological K-theory of categories of matrix factorizations

In this section, we compute the topological K-theory of categories of matrix factorizations in terms of the monodromy invariant vanishing cycle cohomologies. The results are inspired by the work of Preygel
[
Preb
, Section 6]
, Efimov
[
Efi18
]
, Blanc–Robalo–Toën–Vesozzi
[
BRTV18
]
, and Brown–Dyckerhoff
[
BD20
]
.

6.1.
Monodromy invariant vanishing cycles

In this subsection, we discuss the monodromy invariant vanishing cycles.
Let
\mathscr{X}
be a quotient stack
with a regular function
f\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathbb{C}
.
We denote by

\iota\colon\mathscr{X}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}

the (derived) zero locus of
f
.
Recall the vanishing cycle functor
\varphi_{f}
and
the monodromy operator
\mathrm{T}
, see Subsection
2.4
.

Definition 6.1
.

For
F\in D(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}))
,
we define its

monodromy invariant vanishing cycle

\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}(F)\in D(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}_{0}))
to be the cone of
1-\mathrm{T}
:

(6.1)

\displaystyle\varphi_{f}(F)\xrightarrow{1-\mathrm{T}}\varphi_{f}(F)\to\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}(F)\to\varphi_{f}(F)[1].

The push-forward
of
\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}(F)

via
\iota\colon\mathscr{X}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}

is also denoted by
\varphi_{f}(F)
.
For
F=\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}
, we write

\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}:=\varphi^{\rm{inv}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}})

for simplicity.
It is supported on
\mathrm{Crit}(f)\subset\mathscr{X}

if
\mathscr{X}
is smooth.

Definition 6.2
.

We define
H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F))^{\mathrm{inv}}

and
H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F))_{\mathrm{inv}}
to be

\displaystyle H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F))^{\mathrm{inv}}

\displaystyle:=\text{ker}(1-\mathrm{T})\subset H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F)),

\displaystyle H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F))_{\mathrm{inv}}

\displaystyle:=H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F))/\text{image}(1-\mathrm{T}).

Remark 6.3
.

Directly from the above definition, there are
short exact sequences:

(6.2)

0\to H^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F))_{\mathrm{inv}}\to H^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}(F))\to H^{i+1}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F))^{\mathrm{inv}}\to 0.

Moreover, there is a (non-canonical) isomorphism

(6.3)

\displaystyle H^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F))_{\rm{inv}}\cong H^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}(F))^{\rm{inv}}.

Below we assume that
\mathscr{X}
is smooth.
Let
\alpha
be the map

\alpha\colon\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}=\iota^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}\to\iota^{!}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}[2]=\omega_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}[-2\dim\mathscr{X}_{0}]

given by capping with the fundamental class of the quasi-smooth variety
\mathscr{X}_{0}
.
If
f
is the zero map,
then
\mathscr{X}_{0}\cong\mathscr{X}\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[\epsilon]

for
\epsilon
in homological degree
1
. In this case,
the map
\alpha
is defined to be the zero map.

Lemma 6.4
.

There is a distinguished triangle

(6.4)

\iota_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}\xrightarrow{\alpha}\iota_{*}\iota^{!}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}[2]\to\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}\to\iota_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}[1].

Proof.

We recall two distinguished triangles relating the vanishing and cycle functors applied to the constant sheaf.
A reference is
[
Mas
, Chapter 3]
, especially
[
Mas
, pages 24-28]
. The results in loc. cit. are stated for varieties, but they also hold for quotient stacks using the argument in
[
DM20
, Subsection 2.2]
.
By the definition of vanishing cycle sheaves,
there is an exact triangle in
D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}))
:

(6.5)

\iota_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}[-1]\to\psi_{f}[-1]:=\psi_{f}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}[-1]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{can}}\varphi_{f}[-1]:=\varphi_{f}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}[-1]\rightarrow\iota_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}.

By taking the Verdier dual of the above triangle, we obtain the distinguished triangle:

(6.6)

\varphi_{f}[-1]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{var}}\psi_{f}[-1]\to\iota_{*}\iota^{!}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}[1]\rightarrow\varphi_{f}.

We have that
\mathrm{can}\circ\mathrm{var}=1-\mathrm{T}
, where
\mathrm{T}
is the monodromy operator acting on
\varphi_{f}
.

By combining the above
sequences, we obtain the
following diagram where the rows and the columns are distinguished triangles:

(6.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}\iota^{!}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}[2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{can}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{var}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\mathrm{T}}

In the above diagram, the second row is (
6.5
), the second column is (
6.6
), and the third column is (
6.1
).
Therefore the first row is also a distinguished triangle.
∎

6.2.
The Chern character map for matrix factorizations

Let
\mathscr{X}
be a smooth quotient stack with a regular
function
f\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathbb{C}
.
In this subsection, we construct a Chern character map
for the category of matrix factorizations, where
the target is the cohomology of monodromy invariant vanishing cycles.

We first consider the case that
\mathscr{X}
is a smooth quasi-projective variety.
The category
\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f)
is linear over
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathscr{X})
,
and we consider its relative topological K-theory

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))\in\mathrm{Sp}.

Its rationalization gives an object in
D(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}^{\rm{an}}))
.
We note the following lemma:

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{X}
is a smooth quasi-projective variety.
There is a canonical isomorphism
in
D(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}))
:

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}[\beta^{\pm 1}].

Proof.

From the equivalence (
2.7
), there is an exact sequence of dg-categories

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}(\mathscr{X}_{0})\to D^{b}(\mathscr{X}_{0})\to\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f).

Therefore, we obtain the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{K}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathscr{X}_{0}))_{\mathbb{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\rm{top}}(D^{b}(\mathscr{X}_{0}))_{\mathbb{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))_{\mathbb{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\iota_{\ast}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}[\beta^{\pm 1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\iota_{\ast}\iota^{!}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}[\beta^{\pm 1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}[\beta^{\pm 1}].}

Here, the left square commutes by the diagram (
5.9
)
(and Lemma
5.12
in the
f=0
case) and
the vertical isomorphisms
follow from (
4.5
) and Lemma
4.1
.
By taking cofibers of the top arrows in the
\infty
-category

D(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}))
, we obtain the equivalence (
6.8
).
∎

When
\mathscr{X}
is a smooth quasi-projective variety,
we define the Chern character map of
\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f)

by taking the cohomologies of the global sections of (
6.8
) and using Proposition
4.6
:

(6.10)

\displaystyle\operatorname{ch}\colon K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))\to K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}H^{i+2\ast}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}).

In the case that
\mathscr{X}
is a smooth quotient stack, the Chern character map
is defined via approximation by varieties.
Let
\mathscr{X}=X/G
and
V_{n}
be
G
-representations with open subset

U_{n}\subset V_{n}

as in Subsection
5.10
.
Below we use the same symbol
f
to denote the
composition
(X\times U_{n})/G\to\mathscr{X}\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

For a fixed
\ell
, there are isomorphisms for
n\gg 0
:

\displaystyle H^{\ell}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}})\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}H^{\ell}((X\times U_{n})/G,\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}).

Proof.

The lemma follows from the distinguished triangle (
6.4
)
together with the isomorphisms (
5.4
), (
5.5
).
∎

Let
\operatorname{ch}_{V_{n}}
be defined by

\displaystyle\operatorname{ch}_{V_{n}}\colon K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}((X\times U_{n})/G,f))

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}H^{i+2\ast}((X\times U_{n})/G,\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}).

Here the last map is the Chern character map (
6.10
) for the
variety
(X\times U_{n})/G
.
We then define the Chern character map for the stack
\mathscr{X}

(6.11)

\displaystyle\operatorname{ch}\colon K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))\to\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}})

to be the limit as in (
5.7
):

\operatorname{ch}(y):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\operatorname{ch}_{V_{n}}(\mathrm{res}_{V_{n}}(y)).

Let
d:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{X}
.
Similarly to (
5.10
), we
define the filtration

(6.12)

E_{\ell}K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f)):=\mathrm{ch}^{-1}\left(H^{\geqslant 2d-i-2\ell}(\mathscr{X},\varphi^{\mathrm{inv}}_{f})\right).

We obtain cycle maps on the associated graded pieces:

(6.13)

\mathrm{c}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))\to H^{2d-i-2\ell}(\mathscr{X},\varphi^{\mathrm{inv}}_{f}).

We give some compatibility of Chern character maps.
From the exact sequence (
6.9
),
there is a long exact sequence of abelian groups:

(6.14)

\cdots\to K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}_{0})\to G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}_{0})\to K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))\to K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i-1}(\mathscr{X}_{0})\to G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i-1}(\mathscr{X}_{0})\to\cdots.

By the long exact sequence of cohomologies associated with the triangle (
6.4
),
there is a long exact sequence of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces:

(6.15)

\displaystyle\cdots\to H^{2d-i-2}(\mathscr{X}_{0})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\alpha}H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}_{0})\to H^{2d-i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}[-2])=H^{2d-i-2}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}})

\displaystyle\to H^{2d-i-1}(\mathscr{X}_{0})\to H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i-1}(\mathscr{X}_{0})\to\cdots.

The following compatibility of the Chern character maps
is direct from the construction of (
6.10
)
and Lemma
5.12
.

Proposition 6.7
.

The following diagram commutes, where the top sequence is (
6.14
) and the bottom sequence is (
6.15
):

(6.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i-1}(\mathscr{X}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{X}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i-1}(\mathscr{X}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}

6.3.
The Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch theorem for matrix factorizations

We discuss a version of the Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch theorem for categories of matrix factorizations:

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
h\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
be a morphism of smooth quotient stacks,

f\colon\mathscr{Y}\to\mathbb{C}
a regular function,
and set
g:=f\circ h
.
For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(a) The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{Y},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},g))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{Y},\varphi^{\mathrm{inv}}_{f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi^{\mathrm{inv}}_{g}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}

(b) Assume that
h
is proper. Let

\mathrm{td}(T_{h})\in\widetilde{H}^{0}(\mathscr{X}_{0})
be the Todd class
of the virtual tangent bundle
T_{h}
of
h
and let
h^{\prime}_{*}(-):=h_{*}(\mathrm{td}(T_{h})\cdot(-))
.
Then
the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},g))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{Y},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi^{\mathrm{inv}}_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{Y},\varphi^{\mathrm{inv}}_{f}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}_{*}}

Proof.

We only give a proof of (b), as the proof of (a)
is similar.
By the approximation by varieties, we can
assume that
\mathscr{Y}
is a variety.
Then
\mathscr{X}
is also
a variety as
\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
is proper.
Since the vertical arrows in (
6.16
)
are isomorphisms over
\mathbb{Q}

for a variety
\mathscr{X}
,
the proposition
in this case follows from Proposition
6.7
and Theorem
5.7
.
∎

We note the following functoriality of
the associated graded of
topological K-theory of the category of matrix factorizations.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
h\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
be a morphism of smooth quotient stacks of relative dimension
d
. Let
f\colon\mathscr{Y}\to\mathbb{C}
be a regular function, and set
g:=f\circ h
.
Let
\mathscr{X}_{0}
and
\mathscr{Y}_{0}
be the (derived) zero loci of
g
and
f
, respectively.
For
i,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
,
the morphism
h
induces a pullback map:

\displaystyle h^{*}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{Y},f))\to\mathrm{gr}_{\ell+d}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},g)).

If
h
is proper, then there is a pushforward map:

\displaystyle h_{*}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},g))\to\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{Y},f)).

Proof.

The claim follows from Theorem
6.8

and Proposition
5.6
.
∎

For future reference, we also state explicitly the compatibility of the Chern character maps with Knörrer periodicity, which is a particular case of Theorem
6.8
.

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth affine variety with an action of a reductive group, let
\mathscr{X}:=X/G
and consider a regular function
f\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathbb{C}
.
Let
U
be a finite dimensional representation of
G
and consider the natural pairing
w\colon U\times U^{\vee}\to\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mathscr{Y}:=(X\times U\times U^{\vee})/G
and consider the regular function
f+w\colon\mathscr{Y}\to\mathbb{C}
, where
f
and
w
are pulled-back from
X
and
U\times U^{\vee}
, respectively. Consider the natural maps:

X\mathrel{{\mathop{\twoheadleftarrow}\limits}}X\times U\mathrel{{\mathop{\hookrightarrow}\limits}}X\times U\times U^{\vee}

where
v
is the projection and
s(x,u)=(x,u,0)
.
Let
\mathrm{ch}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{ch}\cdot\mathrm{td}(T_{s})
, where
T_{s}
is the relative tangent complex of
s
.
The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{Y},f+w))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{Y},\varphi_{f+w}^{\rm{inv}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}v^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}v^{*}}

Note that the horizontal maps are isomorphisms by the Thom-Sebastiani theorem, see the proofs of Propositions
9.3
and
9.5
. The top horizontal map induces an isomorphisms called Knörrer periodicity
[
Orl06
,
Hir17
]

6.4.
Injectivity of the Chern character map

The Chern character maps (
5.6
), (
5.8
), or (
6.10
) may not be injective when
\mathscr{X}
is a stack. However, they are all isomorphism over
\mathbb{Q}

when
\mathscr{X}
is a variety.
In some cases of interest, we can show that (
6.10
) is injective for
\mathscr{X}
a stack using the following propositions.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
\mathscr{X}
be a smooth quotient stack and let
f\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathbb{C}
be a regular function. Let
\mathbb{S}
be a subcategory of
\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f)
. Assume that there exists a smooth variety
Y
and a morphism
r\colon Y\to\mathscr{X}
such that
r^{*}\colon\mathbb{S}\to\mathrm{MF}(Y,g)
is fully faithful whose image is (left or right) admissible, where
g:=f\circ r
.
For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the Chern character map

\mathrm{ch}\colon K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathbb{S})\to K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))\to\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}})

is injective over
\mathbb{Q}
.
In particular, we have
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathbb{S})<\infty
.

Proof.

The pullback map
r^{*}\colon K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathbb{S})\hookrightarrow K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(Y,g))
is injective
by the admissibility
assumption. The claim then follows from the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathbb{S})_{\mathbb{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X},f))_{\mathbb{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(Y,g))_{\mathbb{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X},\varphi^{\mathrm{inv}}_{f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(Y,\varphi^{\mathrm{inv}}_{g}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{*}}

∎

6.5.
Monodromy invariant vanishing cycles under semiorthogonal decomposition

We will use variant of Lemma
6.5
for semiorthogonal
summands.
Let
\mathscr{X}
be a smooth quasi-projective variety
with a proper morphism
h\colon\mathscr{X}\to B
.
Consider a regular function
g\colon B\to\mathbb{C}
and
its composition with
h
:

\displaystyle f\colon\mathscr{X}\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}B\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\mathbb{C}.

Set
\mathscr{X}_{0}:=f^{-1}(0)
.
Let
D^{b}(\mathscr{X})=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle
be a

B
-linear semiorthogonal decomposition.
It induces semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle D^{b}(\mathscr{X}_{0})=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}\rangle,\ \mathrm{Perf}(\mathscr{X}_{0})=\langle\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rangle,\ D^{\rm{sg}}(\mathscr{X}_{0})=\langle\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\rm{sg}},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\rm{sg}}\rangle

where
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\rm{sg}}=\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}/\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
,
see
[
PTa
, Proposition 2.5]
.

Lemma 6.12
.

There is an isomorphism
\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\rm{top}}(\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\rm{sg}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\rm{top}}(\mathcal{C}_{i})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.2
, Lemma
6.5
, and the fact that the vanishing cycle functor
commutes with proper push-forward, there are isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\rm{top}}(D^{\rm{sg}}(\mathscr{X}_{0}))_{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle\cong h_{\ast}\mathcal{K}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\rm{top}}(D^{\rm{sg}}(\mathscr{X}_{0}))_{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle\cong h_{\ast}\varphi_{f}^{\rm{inv}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\rm{top}}(D^{b}(\mathscr{X}))_{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle\cong\varphi_{g}^{\rm{inv}}\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\rm{top}}(D^{b}(\mathscr{X}))_{\mathbb{Q}}.

It is straightforward to check that, under the above isomorphism,
the decomposition

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\rm{top}}(D^{\rm{sg}}(\mathscr{X}_{0}))_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\rm{top}}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\rm{sg}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{\mathscr{X}}^{\rm{top}}(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\rm{sg}})_{\mathbb{Q}}

is identified with the decomposition applying

\varphi_{g}^{\rm{inv}}
to the decomposition

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\rm{top}}(D^{b}(\mathscr{X}))_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\rm{top}}(\mathcal{C}_{1})_{\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{B}^{\rm{top}}(\mathcal{C}_{2})_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Therefore we obtain the desired isomorphism.
∎

7.
Dimensional reduction

In this section, we show the compatibility
of Koszul equivalences with
the dimensional reduction in cohomology via the Chern character map.
We will use these compatibilities in Subsection
10
to compute the topological K-theory of preprojective quasi-BPS categories using the computation of the topological K-theory of quasi-BPS categories of tripled quivers with potential.

7.1.
Dimensional reduction in cohomology

In this subsection, we review the dimensional reduction theorem in cohomology
[
Dav17
, Theorem A.1]
,
[
KS11
, Subsection 4.8]
.
Recall the setting of Subsection
2.7
,
i.e.
\mathscr{X}
is a smooth quotient stack,

\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{X}
is a vector bundle of rank
r

with a section
s
, and
\mathscr{P}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}

is its derived zero locus.
We will use the notations of the various maps from the diagram (
2.10
):

(7.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}^{\vee}|_{\mathscr{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}_{0}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Theorem 7.1
.

(
[
Dav17
, Theorem A.1]
)

There is a natural isomorphism for
\bullet\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}))
:

(7.2)

\displaystyle\eta_{!}\varphi_{f}[-1]\eta^{*}\bullet\xrightarrow{\sim}\eta_{!}\eta^{*}j_{*}j^{*}\bullet.

Below we explain how to construct an
isomorphism (
7.2
).
For
\bullet\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0}))
, there is a natural isomorphism:

(7.3)

\varphi_{f}[-1]\iota_{*}\bullet\xrightarrow{\cong}\iota_{*}\bullet.

On the other hand, for
\bullet\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}))
,
there is a natural transformation

(7.4)

\eta^{*}\bullet\to\eta^{*}j_{*}j^{*}\bullet

The natural transformations (
7.4
) and (
7.3
) induce a natural transformation for
\bullet\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}))
:

\varphi_{f}[-1]\eta^{*}\bullet\to\varphi_{f}[-1](\eta^{*}j_{*}j^{*}\bullet)=\eta^{*}j_{*}j^{*}\bullet.

The isomorphism (
7.2
) is obtained by
applying
\eta_{!}
to the above natural transformation.

The cohomological version of the dimensional
reduction theorem is obtained from the
isomorphism (
7.2
) as follows.
By taking the Verdier dual of (
7.2
), we obtain

(7.5)

\eta_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime!}\eta^{!}\bullet=\eta_{*}\eta^{!}j_{*}j^{!}\bullet\xrightarrow{\cong}\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}[-1]\eta^{!}\bullet,

which alternatively can be described as applying the functor
\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}[-1]
to the natural transformation

\displaystyle j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime!}\eta^{!}\bullet\to\eta^{!}\bullet

for
\bullet\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}))
.
By applying
\bullet=\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}
and
taking the cohomology of the two sides in (
7.5
), one obtains the
dimensional reduction
isomorphism:

(7.6)

j^{\prime}_{*}\eta^{\prime*}\colon H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{P})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i+2r}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}|_{\mathscr{P}})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2\dim\mathscr{E}-2r-i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}\mathbb{Q}[-1]).

Note that the monodromy action
on the left hand side is trivial.

We give some lemmas on
vanishing cycle cohomologies in the above context.
Recall that
the monodromy invariant vanishing cycle

\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}
is the cone of the map
\alpha\colon\iota_{\ast}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0}}\to\iota_{\ast}\iota^{!}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{E}^{\vee}}[2]
, see (
6.4
).

Lemma 7.2
.

There is a natural injective map:

(7.7)

\gamma\colon H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}[-1])\hookrightarrow H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}[-2]).

Proof.

The isomorphism (
7.5
) factors through:

\eta_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime!}\eta^{!}\bullet\to\eta_{*}\iota_{*}\iota^{!}\eta^{!}\bullet=\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}[-1]\iota_{*}\iota^{!}\eta^{!}\bullet\to\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}[-1]\eta^{!}\bullet.

The second map applied to
\bullet=\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}}
gives
a map

\displaystyle\eta_{*}\iota_{*}\iota^{!}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{E}^{\vee}}[2]\to\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{E}^{\vee}}[1].

From the diagram (
6.7
), the above
map factors through
\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}
, and thus
there are maps whose composition is an isomorphism:

(7.8)

\beta^{\prime}\colon\eta_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}\omega_{\mathscr{E}^{\vee}|_{\mathscr{P}}}\to\eta_{*}\iota_{*}\iota^{!}\omega_{\mathscr{E}^{\vee}}\to\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}[2\dim\mathscr{E}^{\vee}-2]\xrightarrow{\beta}\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}\omega_{\mathscr{E}^{\vee}}[-1].

We let

\beta^{\diamond}
be a composition
in the above sequence

\displaystyle\beta^{\diamond}\colon\eta_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}\omega_{\mathscr{E}^{\vee}|_{\mathscr{P}}}\to\eta_{*}\iota_{*}\iota^{!}\omega_{\mathscr{E}^{\vee}}\to\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}[2\dim\mathscr{E}^{\vee}-2].

The map
\beta^{\diamond}
provides a splitting of the map
\beta
, thus the triangle (
6.1
)
canonically splits to give natural isomorphism:

(7.9)

\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}[-2]\cong\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}[-2]\oplus\eta_{*}\varphi_{f}[-1].

By taking global sections of this isomorphism,
we obtain the desired injection.
∎

We also note the following:

Lemma 7.3
.

The isomorphism (
7.6
)
is given by the composition

(7.10)

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\colon H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{P})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i+2r}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}|_{\mathscr{P}})\to H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i+2r}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0})\to H^{2\dim\mathscr{E}-2r-i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}[-2])

\displaystyle\to H^{2\dim\mathscr{E}-2r-i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}\mathbb{Q}[-1])=H^{2\dim\mathscr{X}-i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}\mathbb{Q}[-1]).

Here the composition of the maps on the top row on (
7.10
) is given by
\beta^{\diamond}
.

Proof.

The lemma is obtained by taking global sections of the complexes in (
7.8
).
∎

7.2.
The Chern character for graded matrix factorizations

The purpose of this subsection is to construct a Chern character map:

(7.11)

\mathrm{ch}\colon K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))\to\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}[-1])

compatible with the Chern character map (
5.16
) for
\mathscr{P}
and the Chern character map (
6.10
) for
\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f)
, see Proposition
7.4
.

We begin with a few preliminaries.
Recall the Koszul equivalence from Theorem
2.2
:

(7.12)

\displaystyle\kappa\colon D^{b}(\mathscr{P})\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\mathrm{MF}^{\rm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f).

Let
l
be the natural closed immersion
l\colon\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{cl}}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{P}
.
Note that the pushforward map induces an equivalence
l_{*}\colon G^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{cl}})\xrightarrow{\sim}G^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathscr{P})
.
Recall the forget-the-grading functor:

(7.13)

\Theta\colon\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f)\to\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f)

and the equivalence from Theorem
2.1
:

\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f)\xrightarrow{\sim}D_{\mathrm{sg}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0}).

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{cl}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\mathrm{sg}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{*}\eta^{\prime*}l_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}\eta^{\prime*}l_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

By the isomorphism (
5.2
), we obtain a commutative diagram

(7.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{sg}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{*}\eta^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}\eta^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta}

By Proposition
2.3
, the top left arrow is
the isomorphism induced by the Koszul equivalence (
7.12
).

Recall the Chern character (
6.11
) and the splitting (
7.10
).
Let
N
be the normal bundle of
\mathscr{P}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{E}^{\vee}
, and
set
\mathrm{ch}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{ch}\cdot\mathrm{td}(N)
.
By noting that
that normal bundle of
\mathscr{E}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{E}
is trivial,
the following diagram commutes by Proposition
5.10
:

(7.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{E}_{0}^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\ast}\eta^{\prime\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{*}\eta^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\diamond}}

Proposition 7.4
.

There is a Chern character
map (
7.11
)
which fits into the commutative diagram

(7.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}[-1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}[-2]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

Here each horizontal arrow is injective over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Moreover the following diagram commutes as well for
the modified Chern character maps
for the immersions of
\mathscr{E}^{\vee}|_{\mathscr{P}}
and
\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{0}
in
\mathscr{E}^{\vee}
:

(7.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i}(\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}[-1]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{*}\eta^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{*}\eta^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Proof.

We define the Chern character map
7.11

to be the unique
map
such that the diagram (
7.17
) commutes.
Let
\Psi
be the map in the diagram (
7.15
).
We have that
\gamma\circ j^{\prime}_{*}\eta^{\prime*}=\beta^{\diamond}
and
\Theta\circ j^{\prime}_{*}\eta^{\prime*}=\Psi
, so the diagram (
7.16
) commutes as well.
By Proposition
7.5
below,
the map
\Theta
is an embedding into the
direct summand, hence it is injective.
∎

The topological K-group
K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\ast}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f)
is a
module over
K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\ast}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C},0)=\Lambda
, where

\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}[\epsilon]

with
\deg\epsilon=-1
, see Subsection
13.2
.
We have used the following computation of a
\Lambda
-module structure on the topological K-theory of a category of matrix factorizations,
which will be proved in Subsection
13.2
.

Proposition 7.5
.

The forget-the-grading functor induces an isomorphism

(7.18)

K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\ast}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\xrightarrow{\cong}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\ast}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))

of
\Lambda
-modules.
Thus for an admissible subcategory

\mathbb{M}\subset D^{b}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee})
,
there is an isomorphism of
\Lambda
-modules:

K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\ast}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathbb{M},f))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\xrightarrow{\cong}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{\ast}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathbb{M},f)).

Similarly to (
5.10
), we define an increasing filtration

\displaystyle E_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))\subset K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))

by the following

E_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f)):=\mathrm{ch}^{-1}\left(H^{\geqslant 2\dim\mathscr{E}^{\vee}-i-2\ell}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}[-1])\right).

We obtain the cycle map

\mathrm{c}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))\to H^{2\dim\mathscr{E}^{\vee}-i-2\ell}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}[-1])

which is an isomorphism by the isomorphism (
5.19
) together with
the commutative diagram (
7.17
).
The following is a corollary of Proposition
6.11
and Proposition
7.4
:

Proposition 7.6
.

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}G^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{gr}_{\ell+r}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{gr}}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{gr}_{\ell+r+1}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},f))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\mathrm{BM}}_{i+2\ell}(\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2\dim\mathscr{X}-i-2\ell}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}[-1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2\dim\mathscr{X}-i-2\ell}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee},\varphi_{f}^{\mathrm{inv}}[-2]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{*}\eta^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}_{*}\eta^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

Proof.

The modified Chern characters
\mathrm{ch}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}
induce the cycle maps
\mathrm{c}
on the associated graded, see Proposition
5.6
.
∎

8.
Topological K-theory of quasi-BPS categories for quivers with potential

In this section, we
give statements of the main results in this paper
on
topological K-theory of quasi-BPS categories
in terms of BPS cohomologies, see Theorem
8.14
. The main step in the proof of Theorem
8.14
is the construction of the cycle map from topological K-theory of quasi-BPS categories to BPS cohomology, see Theorem
8.15
.
In this section, we prove Theorem
8.14

assuming Theorem
8.15
.
The proof of Theorem
8.15
will be given
in the next section.

Before we state Theorem
8.14
, we introduce notation related to quasi-BPS categories and BPS sheaves.
Below
(Q,W)
is a symmetric quiver with
potential
W
.
We use the notation of Subsection
3.1
.

8.1.
Sets of partitions

In this subsection, we introduce certain sets of partitions. In the next subsection, we use these sets to define constructible sheaves whose cohomology are the target of the cycle map from topological K-theory of quasi-BPS categories. We also present some exampels of explicit computations of such sets of partitions.

Let
d=(d^{j})_{j\in I}
be a dimension vector
and let
\delta\in M(d)_{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{d}}
.
For a cocharacter
\lambda
of
T(d)
, recall
the definition of
n_{\lambda}
in (
3.13
).
We introduce the following definition:

Definition 8.1
.

For
\lambda
an antidominant cocharacter of
T(d)
, we define

(8.1)

\varepsilon_{\lambda,\delta}:=\begin{cases}1,\text{ if }n_{\lambda}/2+\langle\lambda,\delta\rangle\in\mathbb{Z},\\
0,\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

For a partition
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
of
d
, we define
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{d},\delta}=1
if
\varepsilon_{\lambda,\delta}=1
for any cocharacter
\lambda
with associated partition
\mathbf{d}
, and define
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{d},\delta}=0
otherwise.
We define
S^{d}_{\delta}
to be the set of partitions
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
of
d

such that
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{d},\delta}=1
. If
\delta=v\tau_{d}
, we use the notation
S^{d}_{v}
instead of
S^{d}_{v\tau_{d}}
.

We have the following another
characterization of
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{d},\delta}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
be a partition of
d
, and
\lambda
an antidominant cocharacter
with associated partition
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
.
Let
\theta_{i}\in\frac{1}{2}M(d_{i})
and
\delta_{i}\in M(d_{i})_{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{d_{i}}}
be defined
by

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\theta_{i}=-\frac{1}{2}R(d)^{\lambda>0}+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{g}(d)^{\lambda>0},\quad\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i}=\delta.

Then we have
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{d},\delta}=1
if
and only if
\langle 1_{d_{i}},\theta_{i}+\delta_{i}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
i
.

Proof.

By the definition, we have

\varepsilon_{\mathbf{d},\delta}=1
if and only
if

\langle\lambda,\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\theta_{i}+\delta_{i})\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}

for all antidominant cocharacter
\lambda

with associated partition
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
.
Therefore, the claim follows.
∎

Below we explain the meaning of the above definition
in terms of categorical Hall product of quasi-BPS categories.
Recall the definition of

\mathbb{M}(d;\delta)
,
\mathbb{S}(d;\delta)
from Subsection
3.7
.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
be a partition
of
d
and define
\theta_{i}
,
\delta_{i}
as in
Lemma
8.2
.
Then the categorical Hall products (
3.7
), (
3.9
)
restrict to the functors

(8.2)

\displaystyle m_{\lambda}\colon\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{M}(d_{i};\theta_{i}+\delta_{i})\to\mathbb{M}(d;\delta),\ m_{\lambda}\colon\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{S}(d_{i};\theta_{i}+\delta_{i})\to\mathbb{S}(d;\delta).

Proof.

Let
\chi_{i}\in M(d_{i})
be dominant weights such that

\chi_{i}+\rho_{i}-(\theta_{i}+\delta_{i})\in\mathbf{W}(d_{i})
.
Here
\rho_{i}
is the half the sum of positive weights of
\mathfrak{g}(d_{i})
.
Let
\chi=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\chi_{i}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\chi+\rho-\delta=-\frac{1}{2}R(d)^{\lambda>0}+\gamma

where
\gamma\in\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{W}(d_{i})
.
The above element lies in the boundary of
\mathbf{W}(d)
.
Then applying the argument of
[
Păda
, Propositions 3.5 and 3.6]
,
one concludes that the categorical Hall product

m_{\lambda}\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k}\Gamma_{G(d_{i})}(\chi_{i})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}(d_{i})}\right)

is generated by
\Gamma_{G(d)}(\chi^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}(d)}

where
\chi^{\prime}+\rho-\delta\in\mathbf{W}(d)
.
(Here in loc. cit. and using the notations used there,
[
Păda
, Proposition 3.6]
is stated
in the case that the
r
-invariant is bigger than
1/2
,
but the case when the
r
-invariant is
1/2
still works).
∎

Remark 8.4
.

The left hand sides in (
8.2
) are non-zero
only if
\langle 1_{d_{i}},\theta_{i}+\delta_{i}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore by Lemma
8.2
, the condition

(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\in S_{\delta}^{d}
is a necessary
condition of the left hand sides of (
8.3
) to be non-zero.
Indeed, this is also a sufficient condition
if
X(d_{i})^{\circ}\neq\emptyset
for all
i
.

We next compute the set of partitions
S_{\delta}^{d}
in some particular examples.

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
Q=(I,E)
be a quiver with an even number of edges between
any two different vertices and an odd number of loops at every vertex.
Let
(d,v)\in\mathbb{N}^{I}\times\mathbb{Z}
.
The set
S^{d}_{v}
consists of partitions
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
such that

\displaystyle v\cdot\underline{d}_{i}/\underline{d}\in\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ for all }1\leqslant i\leqslant k.

In particular, if
\gcd(v,\underline{d})=1
, then
S^{d}_{v}
contains only the one term partition of
d
.

Proof.

Let
d=(d^{a})_{a\in I}\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
.
Note that
n_{\lambda}=\langle\lambda,\mathbb{L}^{\lambda>0}_{\mathscr{X}(d)}|_{0}\rangle
is even by the assumption of
Q
. Then
\varepsilon_{\mathbf{d},v\tau_{d}}=1
if and only if
\langle\lambda,v\tau_{d}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
for all cocharacters
\lambda
with associated partition
\mathbf{d}
.
We write
\lambda=(\lambda^{a})_{a\in I}
, where
\lambda^{a}\colon\mathbb{C}^{*}\to T(d^{a})
is a cocharacter

\displaystyle\lambda(t)=(t^{m_{1}},\ldots,t^{m_{1}},t^{m_{2}},\ldots,t^{m_{2}},\ldots,t^{m_{k}}),

where
m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
appears
d^{a}_{i}
-times, and

m_{1}>\cdots>m_{k}
.
Then the condition
\langle\lambda,v\tau_{d}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}

is equivalent to

v/\underline{d}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}\underline{d}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}

for all tuples of integers
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}

with
m_{1}>\cdots>m_{k}
,
which implies the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
Q
be a quiver with one vertex and
2e
-loops for some
e\geqslant 1
.
Then
S_{v}^{d}
consists of partitions
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
such that, for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}d_{i}\left(\sum_{j<i}d_{j}-\sum_{j>i}d_{j}\right)+\frac{vd_{i}}{d}\in\mathbb{Z}.

Moreover,

S_{v}^{d}=\{d\}
if and only if
\gcd(d,v)=1
and
d\not\equiv 2\pmod{4}
,
or if
\gcd(d,v)=2
and
d\equiv 2\pmod{4}
.

Proof.

In the notation of the proof of Proposition
8.5
,
we have
n_{\lambda}/2+\langle\lambda,v\tau_{d}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}

if and only if

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m_{i}(2e-1)d_{i}\left(\sum_{j<i}d_{j}-\sum_{j>i}d_{j}\right)+\frac{vm_{i}d_{i}}{d}\in\mathbb{Z}

for all tuples of pairwise distinct integers
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
,
which implies the first result.

By the above description of
S_{v}^{d}
,
if
d=d_{1}+\cdots+d_{k}
is an element of
S_{v}^{d}
, then
the 2-length partition
d=d_{1}+d_{2}^{\prime}

with
d_{2}^{\prime}=d_{2}+\cdots+d_{k}
is also an element of
S_{v}^{d}
.
Therefore the second claim follows from Lemma
8.7
below.
∎

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
be an odd number.
For
(d,v)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
d>0
,
there is no decomposition

(d,v)=(d_{1},v_{1})+(d_{2},v_{2})

in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with

d_{i}>0

satisfying

(8.3)

\displaystyle\frac{w_{1}}{d_{1}}=\frac{w_{2}}{d_{2}},\ w_{1}:=v_{1}-\frac{m}{2}d_{1}d_{2},\ w_{2}:=v_{2}+\frac{m}{2}d_{1}d_{2}

if and only if

\gcd(d,v)=1
and
d\not\equiv 2\pmod{4}
,
or if
\gcd(d,v)=2
and
d\equiv 2\pmod{4}
.

Proof.

Note that there is a decomposition (
8.3
)
if and only if we have the decomposition in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
:

(8.4)

\displaystyle(2d,2v)=(2d_{1},2w_{1})+(2d_{2},2w_{2})

such that

w_{1}/d_{1}=v/d
and

v_{1}=w_{1}+md_{1}d_{2}/2\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We write
(d,v)=k(d_{0},v_{0})
for
k>0
and
(d_{0},v_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

such that
(d_{0},v_{0})

is coprime. If
k\geqslant 3
, there is always
a decomposition (
8.4
)
by
(2d_{1},2w_{1})=4(d_{0},v_{0})
,

(2d_{2},2w_{2})=(2k-4)(d_{0},v_{0})
.
So we focus on the case that
k=1,2
.

Suppose that
k=1
.
Then the right hand side of (
8.4
)
has divisibility
2
, so if there is a decomposition (
8.4
)
then

(2d_{i},2w_{i})=(d,v)
.
It follows that
d
is even and
v
is odd.
Then we have

\displaystyle v_{1}=\frac{v}{2}+\frac{md^{2}}{8}

and it is an integer if and only if
d\equiv 2\pmod{4}
.

Suppose that
k=2
and there is a decomposition (
8.4
).
Then there is
1\leqslant j\leqslant 3

such that
(2d_{1},2w_{1})=j(d_{0},w_{0})

and
(2d_{2},2w_{2})=(4-j)(d_{0},w_{0})
. We have

\displaystyle v_{1}=\frac{jv_{0}}{2}+\frac{j(4-j)md_{0}^{2}}{8}

and it is an integer only if
d_{0}
is even. Conversely, if
d_{0}
is even,
then
v_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
for
j=2
.
∎

Proposition 8.8
.

For any symmetric quiver
Q=(I,E)
, there is
\delta\in M(d)_{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{d}}
such that

S_{\delta}^{d}=\{d\}
.

Proof.

The condition
\{d\}\in S_{\delta}^{d}
is equivalent to that

\langle 1_{d},\delta\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In the case that
\lvert I\rvert=1
, then
\delta=v\tau_{d}
for some
v\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By Proposition
8.5
and Lemma
8.7
, we have

S_{v}^{d}=\{d\}
for either
v=1
or
v=0
.

In the case that
\lvert I\rvert\geqslant 2
,
we first take
\delta\in M(d)_{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{d}}

such that
v:=\langle 1_{d},\lambda\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
, and set
\delta^{\prime}=\delta-v\tau_{d}
.
Then
\langle 1_{d},\delta^{\prime}\rangle=0
.
Note that the space of

\delta^{\prime}\in M(d)_{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{d}}
satisfying
\langle 1_{d},\delta^{\prime}\rangle=0

is
\mathbb{R}^{\lvert I\rvert-1}
, hence uncountable.
Therefore for a very general choice of
\delta^{\prime}
,
we have
n_{\lambda}/2+\langle\lambda,\delta\rangle\notin\mathbb{Z}

for any
\lambda
such that the hyperplane

H_{\lambda}:=\{\langle\lambda,\delta^{\prime}\rangle=0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\lvert I\rvert-1}

is of codimension one. We have

H_{\lambda}=\mathbb{R}^{\lvert I\rvert-1}
if and only
if the associated partition

d=d_{1}+\cdots+d_{k}
is proportional to
d
, i.e.
by writing
d=md_{0}
with
d_{0}
a primitive dimension
vector and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
d_{i}=m_{i}d_{0}

where
m=m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k}
is a partition of
m
.
Then we have

(8.5)

\displaystyle\frac{n_{\lambda}}{2}+\langle\lambda,\delta\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\nu,(R(d_{0})-\mathfrak{g}(d_{0}))\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)^{\nu>0}\right\rangle+\langle\nu,v\tau_{m}\rangle.

Here
W
is a
m
-dimensional vector space
and
\nu
is an antidominant cocharacter of the maximal
torus of
GL(W)
with associated partition
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
.
Therefore, we are reduced to the same computation
for the one-vertex case
with
e
-loops, where

e=\dim R(d_{0})-\dim\mathfrak{g}(d_{0})+1
,
and
(
8.5
) is not an integer
for any such
\lambda
for
v=1
if
e
is odd and

d\not\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, or for
v=0
if

e
is even and
d\equiv 2\pmod{4}
.
∎

8.2.
BPS sheaves

Let us consider the stack of
Q
-representations of
dimension
d
and its good moduli space:

\pi_{d}\colon\mathscr{X}(d):=R(d)/G(d)\to X(d):=R(d)/\!\!/G(d).

Recall that the potential
W
gives a
function
\mathop{\rm Tr}W\colon\mathscr{X}(d)\to\mathbb{C}
.
It factors through the good moduli space map

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Tr}W\colon\mathscr{X}(d)\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}X(d)\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\mathbb{C}.

The affine variety
X(d)
parametrizes
semisimple
Q
-representations.
Let
X(d)^{\circ}\subset X(d)

be the open subset consisting of
simple
Q
-representations.
Following
[
DM20
]
, the BPS sheaf
is defined as follows:

Definition 8.9
.

(
[
DM20
]
)
The BPS sheaf
\mathcal{BPS}_{d}\in\mathrm{Perv}(X(d))
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}_{d}:=\begin{cases}\varphi_{\mathrm{Tr}\,W}\mathrm{IC}_{X(d)}[-1],\text{ if }X(d)^{\circ}\neq\emptyset,\\
0,\text{ if }X(d)^{\circ}=\emptyset.\end{cases}

Recall the definition of monodromy invariant
vanishing cycle in Definition
6.1
.
We define the
monodromy invariant BPS sheaf

as follows:

Definition 8.10
.

We define
\mathcal{BPS}_{d}^{\rm{inv}}\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X(d)))
to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}_{d}^{\rm{inv}}:=\begin{cases}\varphi^{\rm{inv}}_{\mathrm{Tr}\,W}\mathrm{IC}_{X(d)}[-1],\text{ if }X(d)^{\circ}\neq\emptyset,\\
0,\text{ if }X(d)^{\circ}=\emptyset.\end{cases}

We define some direct sums of BPS sheaves
associated with partitions of
d
.
For a partition
A=(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
of
d
, its length is defined to be
\ell(A):=k
. Assume the set
\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}\}=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{s}\}
has cardinality
s
and that, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant s
, there are
m_{i}
elements in
\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}\}
equal to
e_{i}
.
We define the following maps,
given by the direct sums of semisimple

Q
-representations

\displaystyle\oplus_{i}\colon X(e_{i})^{\times m_{i}}\to X(m_{i}e_{i}),\ \oplus^{\prime}\colon\times_{i=1}^{s}X(m_{i}e_{i})\to X(d).

The above maps are finite maps.
We define the following perverse sheaves:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}^{m_{i}}\big(\mathcal{BPS}_{e_{i}}\big)

\displaystyle:=\oplus_{i,\ast}\left(\mathcal{BPS}_{e_{i}}^{\boxtimes m_{i}}\right)^{\mathfrak{S}_{m_{i}}}\in\mathrm{Perv}(X(m_{i}e_{i})),

(8.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}_{A}

\displaystyle:=\oplus^{\prime}_{\ast}\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{s}\mathrm{Sym}^{m_{i}}(\mathcal{BPS}_{e_{i}})\right)\in\mathrm{Perv}(X(d)).

Definition 8.11
.

For
\delta\in M(d)_{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{d}}
,
we define the following direct sum of symmetric
products of BPS sheaves:

(8.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta}

\displaystyle:=\bigoplus_{A\in S^{d}_{\delta}}\mathcal{BPS}_{A}[-\ell(A)]\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X(d))).

For
\delta=v\tau_{d}
, we write

\mathcal{BPS}_{d,v}:=\mathcal{BPS}_{d,v\tau_{d}}
for simplicity.

As we will see in (
8.22
), the complexes
\mathcal{BPS}_{A}
and
\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta}

are direct summands of
\pi_{d\ast}\varphi_{\mathop{\rm Tr}W}\mathrm{IC}_{\mathscr{X}(d)}[-1]
preserved by
1-\mathrm{T}
.
We define the monodromy invariant versions as follows:

Definition 8.12
.

We define

\mathcal{BPS}_{A}^{\mathrm{inv}},\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta}^{\mathrm{inv}}\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X(d)))
by the exact triangles:

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}_{A}^{\mathrm{inv}}[-1]\to\mathcal{BPS}_{A}\xrightarrow{1-\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{BPS}_{A}\to\mathcal{BPS}_{A}^{\mathrm{inv}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta}^{\mathrm{inv}}[-1]\to\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta}\xrightarrow{1-\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta}\to\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta}^{\mathrm{inv}}.

Remark 8.13
.

Alternatively, by the Thom-Sebastiani theorem
we have another description. Let
\mathrm{IC}_{A}
be the sheaf defined similarly to
\mathcal{BPS}_{A}

without potential. Then
we have, see Lemma
8.24

\displaystyle\mathcal{BPS}_{A}=\varphi_{\mathrm{Tr}\,W}\mathrm{IC}_{A}[-1],\ \mathcal{BPS}_{A}^{\rm{inv}}=\varphi_{\mathop{\rm Tr}W}^{\rm{inv}}\mathrm{IC}_{A}[-1].

If
A
is a trivial partition
A=\{d\}
, then

\mathcal{BPS}_{A}^{\rm{inv}}=\mathcal{BPS}_{d}^{\rm{inv}}

by definition.

8.3.
Statement of the main theorems

In this subsection, we state the main theorems of the paper and we note a few corollaries.
We use the notation from the previous subsection.

Let us consider the Chern character map (
6.10
):

(8.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\colon K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta))\to K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X}(d),\mathrm{Tr}\,W))\to\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathscr{X}(d),\varphi_{\mathrm{Tr}\,W}^{\mathrm{inv}}).

Here the first map is injective over
\mathbb{Q}

by Proposition
6.11
.
Recall (
6.12
) and define the filtration:

E_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta)):=K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta))\cap E_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathrm{MF}(\mathscr{X}(d),\mathrm{Tr}\,W))\subset K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta)).

There is an injective cycle map on the associated graded pieces:

(8.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{c}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta))

\displaystyle\to H^{2\dim\mathscr{X}(d)-2\ell-i}(\mathscr{X}(d),\varphi_{\mathrm{Tr}\,W}^{\mathrm{inv}})

\displaystyle=H^{\dim\mathscr{X}(d)-2\ell-i}(\mathscr{X}(d),\varphi_{\mathrm{Tr}\,W}^{\mathrm{inv}}\,\mathrm{IC}_{\mathscr{X}(d)}),

where we used
\varphi_{\mathrm{Tr}\,W}\mathrm{IC}_{\mathscr{X}(d)}=\varphi_{\mathrm{Tr}\,W}[\dim\mathscr{X}(d)]
in the computation
of the cohomological degree.
The following is the main result of this section:

Theorem 8.14
.

The cycle map (
8.9
) induces an isomorphisms for
i,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
:

(8.10)

\displaystyle\mathrm{c}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}K_{i}^{\mathrm{top}}\left(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta)\right)_{\mathbb{Q}}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\dim\mathscr{X}(d)-2\ell-i}(X(d),\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta}^{\mathrm{inv}}[1]).

Note that, by Proposition
8.8
, there exists
\delta
satisfying

S_{\delta}^{d}=\{d\}
.
In this case,
we regard
\mathbb{S}(d;\delta)
as a categorification of the monodromy invariant BPS cohomology of
(Q,W)
.
The main part of proving Theorem
8.14
is the construction of a cycle map from the topological K-theory of quasi-BPS categories to BPS cohomologies,
whose proof will be given in the next section.

Theorem 8.15
.

Let
(Q,W)
be a symmetric quiver with
potential.
Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
,
\delta\in M(d)^{W_{d}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, and
i,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The cycle map (
8.9
) induces a map:

(8.11)

\mathrm{c}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}K_{i}^{\mathrm{top}}\left(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta)\right)\to H^{\dim\mathscr{X}(d)-2\ell-i}\left(X(d),\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta}^{\mathrm{inv}}[1]\right).

We note that the cycle map (
8.11
) is injective,
as (
8.9
) is injective
and the map (
8.11
) is
a map to the direct summand in the right hand side
of (
8.9
).
We mention a numerical corollary of Theorems
8.14
and
8.15
.
Before this, we have the following lemma:

Lemma 8.16
.

There is an equality

(8.12)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta))_{\mathbb{Q}}=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathrm{gr}_{\ast}K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta))_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Proof.

The Chern
character map (
8.8
) is injective
over
\mathbb{Q}
by
Proposition
6.11
and Theorem
3.5
.
Thus
E_{\ell}K_{i}^{\rm{top}}(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta))_{\mathbb{Q}}=0
for
\ell\ll 0
,
so we obtain the equality (
8.12
).
∎

Corollary 8.17
.

Let
(Q,W)
be a symmetric quiver with
potential. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
and
\delta\in M(d)_{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{d}}
.
Then there is an equality

(8.13)

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}(\mathbb{S}(d;\delta))_{\mathbb{Q}}=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}H^{\ast}(X(d),\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta})^{\mathrm{inv}}.

Proof of Corollary
8.17
from Theorems
8.14
,
8.15
.

Note that there is a (non-canonical) isomorphism

(8.14)

\displaystyle H^{\bullet}(X(d),\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta})^{\mathrm{inv}}\cong H^{\bullet}(X(d),\mathcal{BPS}_{d,\delta})_{\mathrm{inv}}.

The claim then follows from Theorem
8.14
and Lemma
8.16
.
∎

The result of
Corollary
1.8
follows easily from Theorem
8.14
, as follows:

Proof of Corollary
1.8
.

Note that Proposition
8.5
implies that
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\in S^{d}_{v}
if and only if
\underline{d}/\gcd(\underline{d},v)
divides
\underline{d}_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
. Then

S^{d}_{v}=S^{d}_{v^{\prime}}
for
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\gcd(\underline{d},v)=\gcd(\underline{d},v^{\prime})
.
The statement then follows from Theorem
8.14
.
∎

We finish this subsection by the discussion of the zero potential case of Theorem
8.14
.
In this case
\mathcal{BPS}_{d}=\mathrm{IC}_{X(d)}
,
and we denote by
\mathrm{IH}^{\bullet}(X(d)):=H^{\bullet}\big(X(d),\mathrm{IC}_{X(d)}\big)
. By Theorem
8.14
and Proposition
13.2
, we obtain the following:

Theorem 8.18
.

Let
Q=(I,E)
be a symmetric quiver,
let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{I}

and
\delta\in M(d)_{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{d}}

such that
S_{\delta}^{d}=\{d\}
.
For
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, the cycle map induces an isomorphism:

\mathrm{c}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\ell}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{0}(\mathbb{M}(d;\delta))_{\mathbb{Q}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{IH}^{\dim\mathscr{X}(d)-2\ell-1}(X(d)).

In particular, we have

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}K^{\mathrm{top}}_{0}(\mathbb{M}(d;\delta))_{\mathbb{Q}}=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathrm{IH}^{\ast}(X(d)).

Remark 8.19
.

Note that
H^{\mathrm{odd}}(\mathscr{X}(d))=H^{\mathrm{odd}-\dim\mathscr{X}(d)}(\mathscr{X}(d),\mathrm{IC}_{\mathscr{X}(d)})=0
. We then have that
\mathrm{IH}^{\mathrm{even}-\dim\mathscr{X}(d)}(X(d))=0
because
\mathrm{IC}_{X(d)}[-1]
is a direct summand of
R\pi_{d\ast}\mathrm{IC}_{\mathscr{X}(d)}
, see (
8.19
); alternatively, the vanishing
\mathrm{IH}^{\mathrm{even}-\dim\mathscr{X}(d)}(X(d))=0
follows from Kirwan surjectivity.

For
\delta\in M(d)_{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{d}}

with
S_{\delta}^{d}=\{d\}
, we
regard
\mathbb{M}(d;\delta)
as a categorification of the intersection cohomology of
X(d)
. Note that, in general,
X(d)
is a singular variety, and it is not straightforward to construct (natural) categorifications of intersection cohomology for a singular variety.

Example 8.20
.

We discuss the isomorphism in Theorem
8.14
in a simple example.
Let
Q
be a quiver with one vertex and
(2e+1)
-loops, and
consider the case of
d=2
and
\delta=\tau_{2}
. Let
V
be a two dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-vector space.
The moduli stack is

\mathscr{X}(2)=\mathfrak{gl}(2)^{\oplus(2e+1)}/GL(2)=\mathrm{End}(V)^{\oplus(2e+1)}/\mathrm{Aut}(V),

and we have

\displaystyle K_{0}^{\rm{top}}(\mathscr{X}(2))=\mathbb{Z}[q_{1}^{\pm 1},q_{2}^{\pm 1}]^{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}.

The quasi-BPS category
\mathbb{M}(2)_{1}
is generated by

\mathrm{Sym}^{a}(V)\otimes(\det V)^{1-a}
for
0\leqslant a\leqslant e-1
,
so we have

\displaystyle K^{\rm{top}}_{0}(\mathbb{M}(2)_{1})=\bigoplus_{a=0}^{e-1}\mathbb{Z}\cdot(q_{1}^{a}q_{2}^{1-a}+q_{1}^{a-1}q_{2}^{2-a}+\cdots+q_{1}^{1-a}q_{2}^{a}).

The associated graded has the description:

(8.15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\ast}K^{\rm{top}}_{0}(\mathbb{M}(2)_{1})=\bigoplus_{a=0}^{e-1}\mathbb{Z}\cdot q_{1}^{-a}q_{2}^{-a}(q_{1}+q_{2})(q_{1}-q_{2})^{a}.

On the other hand, we will recall
in Theorem
8.23

the decomposition theorem of intersection cohomologies of
moduli stacks of representations of quivers by Davison-Meinhardt
[
DM20
]
.
In the case of this example, the above decomposition is

\displaystyle\mathrm{IH}^{\ast}(\mathscr{X}(2))=

\displaystyle\mathrm{IH}^{\ast}(X(2))\otimes(\mathbb{Q}[-1]\oplus\mathbb{Q}[-3]\oplus\cdots)

\displaystyle\oplus\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(\mathrm{IH}^{\ast}(X(1))\otimes(\mathbb{Q}[-1]\oplus\mathbb{Q}[-3]\oplus\cdots)).

We have
\mathrm{IH}^{\ast}(\mathscr{X}(2))=\mathbb{Q}[\hbar_{1},\hbar_{2}]^{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}
and
the above decomposition is
(ignoring grading)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}[\hbar_{1},\hbar_{2}]^{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}=

\displaystyle\oplus_{a=0}^{e-1}\mathbb{Q}\cdot(\hbar_{1}-\hbar_{2})^{2a}\otimes(\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(\hbar_{1}+\hbar_{2})\oplus\cdots)

\displaystyle\oplus_{m_{1}\geqslant m_{2}\geqslant 0}\mathbb{Q}(\hbar_{1}-\hbar_{2})^{2e}(\hbar_{1}^{m_{1}}\hbar_{2}^{m_{2}}+\hbar_{1}^{m_{2}}\hbar_{2}^{m_{1}}),

where
\mathrm{IH}^{\ast}(X(2))
is

(8.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{IH}^{\ast}(X(2))=\bigoplus_{a=0}^{e-1}\mathbb{Q}\cdot(\hbar_{1}-\hbar_{2})^{2a}.

The cycle map sends summands of (
8.15
) to summands of (
8.16
) as follows:

\displaystyle c\colon q_{1}^{-a}q_{2}^{-a}(q_{1}+q_{2})(q_{1}-q_{2})^{a}\mapsto 2(\hbar_{1}-\hbar_{2})^{2a}.

Note that we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(\mathrm{IH}^{\ast}(X(1))[-1])[-2m_{1}-2m_{2}]=\mathbb{Q}(\hbar_{1}-\hbar_{2})^{2e}(\hbar_{1}^{m_{1}}\hbar_{2}^{m_{2}}+\hbar_{1}^{m_{2}}\hbar_{2}^{m_{1}})

via cohomological Hall product (
3.8
), see the explicit formula in
[
KS11
, Theorem 2]
for the cohomological Hall product.

8.4.
The decomposition theorem

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
and recall the construction of framed quivers
Q^{\alpha f}
from Subsection
3.9
.

We review the explicit computation of summands in the BBDG decomposition theorem
[
BBD82
]
for the pushforward of the constant sheaf along the maps:

\displaystyle\pi_{\alpha f,d}\colon\mathscr{X}^{\alpha f}(d)^{\text{ss}}\to X(d),\ \pi_{d}\colon\mathscr{X}(d)\to X(d)

due to Meinhardt–Reineke
[
MR19
]
and Davison–Meinhardt
[
DM20
]
.
The maps
\pi_{\alpha f,d}
“approximate” the map
\pi_{d}
, see
[
DM20
, Subsection 4.1]
.
The computation of
\pi_{d*}\mathrm{IC}_{\mathscr{X}(d)}
is deduced from the computation of
\pi_{\alpha f,d*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathscr{X}^{\alpha f}(d)^{\mathrm{ss}}}[\dim\mathscr{X}(d)]
.

We introduce some objects of
D^{b}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{Q}}(X(d)))
,
which generalize the construction in Subsection
8.2
.
Let
A
be a tuplet
(e_{i},m_{i,a})
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant s
and for
a\geqslant 0
, with
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}^{s}
pairwise distinct and
m_{i,a}\geqslant 0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{a\geqslant 0}e_{i}m_{i,a}=d.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be the set of all such tuplets
A
and let
\mathscr{P}_{\alpha}\subset\mathscr{P}
be the subset of such tuplets with
m_{i,a}=0
for
a\geqslant\alpha e_{i}
. Note that each
A
